Assume that the data  $\pmb{x}$  lies on the product of  $Q$  spheres,

$$
\boldsymbol {x} = \left(\boldsymbol {x} ^ {(1)}, \dots , \boldsymbol {x} ^ {(Q)}\right) \in \prod_ {q \in [ Q ]} \mathbb {S} ^ {d _ {q} - 1} (r _ {q}),
$$

where  $d_q = d^{\eta_q}$  and  $r_q = d^{(\eta_q + \kappa_q) / 2}$ . Let  $\pmb{d} = (d_{1},\dots,d_{q}) = (d^{\eta_{1}},\dots,d^{\eta_{q}})$  and  $\kappa = (\kappa_1,\ldots ,\kappa_Q)$ , where  $\eta_q > 0$  and  $\kappa_q\geq 0$  for  $q = 1,\dots ,Q$ . We will denote this space

$$
\mathrm {P S} _ {\kappa} ^ {d} = \prod_ {q \in [ Q ]} \mathbb {S} ^ {d _ {q} - 1} (r _ {q}). \tag {38}
$$

Furthermore, assume that the data is generated following the uniform distribution on  $\mathrm{PS}_{\kappa}^{d}$ , i.e.

$$
\boldsymbol {x} ^ {\text {i . i . d .}} \sim \operatorname {U n i f} \left(\mathrm {P S} _ {\boldsymbol {\kappa}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}}\right) = \bigotimes_ {q \in [ Q ]} \operatorname {U n i f} \left(\mathbb {S} ^ {d _ {q} - 1} \left(r _ {q}\right)\right) \equiv \mu_ {\boldsymbol {d}} ^ {\boldsymbol {\kappa}}. \tag {39}
$$

We have  $\pmb{x} \in \mathbb{R}^D$  and  $\| \pmb{x} \|_2 = R$  where  $D = d^{\eta_1} + \ldots + d^{\eta_Q}$  and  $R = (d^{\eta_1 + \kappa_1} + \ldots + d^{\eta_Q + \kappa_Q})^{1/2}$ .

We will make the following assumption that will simplify the proofs. Denote

$$
\xi \equiv \max  _ {q \in [ Q ]} \left\{\eta_ {q} + \kappa_ {q} \right\}, \tag {40}
$$

then  $\xi$  is attained on only one of the sphere, whose coordinate will be denoted  $q_{\xi}$ , i.e.  $\xi = \eta_{q_{\xi}} + \kappa_{q_{\xi}}$  and  $\eta_q + \kappa_q < \xi$  for  $q \neq q_{\xi}$ .

Let  $\sigma : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$  be an activation function and  $(\boldsymbol{w}_i)_{i \in [N]} \sim_{iid} \mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{D-1})$  the weights. We introduce the random feature function class

$$
\mathcal {F} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\boldsymbol {W}) = \left\{\hat {f} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\boldsymbol {x}; \boldsymbol {a}) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} a _ {i} \sigma (\langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} \rangle \sqrt {D} / R): a _ {i} \in \mathbb {R}, \forall i \in [ N ] \right\},
$$

and the neural tangent function class

$$
\mathcal {F} _ {\mathrm {N T}} (\boldsymbol {W}) = \left\{\hat {f} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\boldsymbol {x}; \boldsymbol {a}) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \langle \boldsymbol {a} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} \rangle \sigma^ {\prime} (\langle \boldsymbol {w} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} \rangle \sqrt {D} / R): \quad \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {D}, \forall i \in [ N ] \right\}.
$$

We will denote  $\pmb{\theta}_i = \sqrt{D}\pmb{w}_i$ . Notice that the normalization in the definition of the function class ensures that the scalar product  $\langle \pmb{x}, \pmb{\theta}_i \rangle / R$  is of order 1. This corresponds to normalizing the data.

We consider the approximation of  $f$  by functions in function classes  $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RF}}(\Theta)$  and  $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NT}}(\Theta)$ .

# C.2 Reparametrization

Recall  $(\pmb{\theta}_i)_{i\in [N]}\sim \mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{D - 1}(\sqrt{D}))$  independently. We decompose  $\pmb {\theta}_i = (\pmb {\theta}_i^{(1)},\dots ,\pmb {\theta}_i^{(Q)})$  into  $Q$  sections corresponding to the  $d_{q}$  coordinates associated to the  $q$ -th sphere. Let us consider the following reparametrization of  $(\pmb{\theta}_i)_{i\in [N]}\stackrel {i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{D - 1}(\sqrt{D}))$ :

$$
\left(\overline {{\pmb {\theta}}} _ {i} ^ {(1)}, \dots , \overline {{\pmb {\theta}}} _ {i} ^ {(Q)}, \tau_ {i} ^ {(1)}, \dots , \tau_ {i} ^ {(Q)}\right),
$$

where

$$
\overline {{\pmb {\theta}}} _ {i} ^ {(q)} \equiv \sqrt {d _ {q}} \pmb {\theta} _ {i} ^ {(q)} / \| \pmb {\theta} _ {i} ^ {(q)} \| _ {2}, \quad \tau_ {i} ^ {(q)} \equiv \| \pmb {\theta} _ {i} ^ {(q)} \| _ {2} / \sqrt {d _ {q}}, \quad \mathrm {f o r} q = 1, \ldots , Q.
$$

Hence

$$
\pmb {\theta} _ {i} = \left(\tau_ {i} ^ {(1)} \cdot \overline {{\pmb {\theta}}} _ {i} ^ {(1)}, \dots , \tau_ {i} ^ {(Q)} \cdot \overline {{\pmb {\theta}}} _ {i} ^ {(Q)}\right).
$$

It is easy to check that the variables  $(\overline{\pmb{\theta}}^{(1)},\dots ,\overline{\pmb{\theta}}^{(Q)})$  are independent and independent of  $(\tau_i^{(1)},\ldots ,\tau_i^{(Q)})$ , and verify

$$
\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}} _ {i} ^ {(q)} \sim \mathrm {U n i f} (\mathbb {S} ^ {d _ {q} - 1} (\sqrt {d _ {q}})), \quad \tau_ {i} ^ {(q)} \sim d _ {q} ^ {- 1 / 2} \sqrt {\mathrm {B e t a} \left(\frac {d _ {q}}{2} , \frac {D - d _ {q}}{2}\right)}, \quad \mathrm {f o r} q = 1, \ldots , Q.
$$

We will denote  $\overline{\pmb{\theta}}_i\equiv (\overline{\pmb{\theta}}_i^{(1)},\dots ,\overline{\pmb{\theta}}_i^{(Q)})$  and  $\pmb {\tau}_i\equiv (\tau_i^{(1)},\dots ,\tau_i^{(Q)})$  . With these notations, we have

$$
\overline {{\pmb {\theta}}} _ {i} \in \prod_ {q \in [ Q ]} \mathbb {S} ^ {d _ {q} - 1} (\sqrt {d _ {q}}) \equiv \mathrm {P S} ^ {\pmb {d}},
$$

where  $\mathrm{PS}^d$  is the 'normalized space of product of spheres', and

$$
\left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}} _ {i}\right) _ {i \in [ N ]} \stackrel {i. i. d.} {\sim} \bigotimes_ {q \in [ Q ]} \mathrm {U n i f} (\mathbb {S} ^ {d _ {q} - 1} (\sqrt {d _ {q}})) \equiv \mu_ {\boldsymbol {d}}.
$$

Similarly, we will denote the rescaled data  $\overline{\pmb{x}}\in \mathrm{PS}^d$

$$
\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} = \left(\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} ^ {(1)}, \dots , \overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} ^ {(Q)}\right) \sim \bigotimes_ {q \in [ Q ]} \mathrm {U n i f} (\mathbb {S} ^ {d _ {q} - 1} (\sqrt {d _ {q}})),
$$

obtained by taking  $\overline{\pmb{x}}^{(q)} = \sqrt{d_q}\pmb{x}^{(q)} / r_q = d^{-\kappa_q / 2}\pmb{x}^{(q)}$  for each  $q\in [Q]$

The proof will proceed as follows: first, noticing that  $\tau^{(q)}$  concentrates around 1 for every  $q = 1,\ldots ,Q$ , we will restrict ourselves without loss of generality to the following high probability event

$$
\mathcal {P} _ {d, N, \varepsilon} \equiv \left\{\Theta \Big | \tau_ {i} ^ {(q)} \in [ 1 - \varepsilon , 1 + \varepsilon ], \forall i \in [ N ], \forall q \in [ Q ] \right\} \subset \mathbb {S} ^ {D - 1} (\sqrt {D}) ^ {N},
$$

where  $\varepsilon > 0$  will be chosen sufficiently small. Then, we rewrite the activation function

$$
\sigma (\langle \cdot , \cdot \rangle / R): \mathbb {S} ^ {D - 1} (\sqrt {D}) \times \mathrm {P S} _ {\kappa} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \to \mathbb {R},
$$

as a function, for a random  $\pmb{\tau}$  (but close to  $(1,\dots ,1))$

$$
\sigma_ {d, \tau}: \mathrm {P S} ^ {d} \times \mathrm {P S} ^ {d} \to \mathbb {R},
$$

given for  $\pmb {\theta} = (\overline{\pmb{\theta}},\pmb {\tau})$  by

$$
\sigma_ {\pmb {d}, \pmb {\tau}} \left(\{\langle \overline {{\pmb {\theta}}} ^ {(q)}, \overline {{\pmb {x}}} ^ {(q)} \rangle / \sqrt {d _ {q}} \} _ {q \in [ Q ]}\right) = \sigma \left(\sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} \frac {\tau^ {(q)} r _ {q}}{R} \cdot \frac {\langle \overline {{\pmb {\theta}}} ^ {(q)} , \overline {{\pmb {x}}} ^ {(q)} \rangle}{\sqrt {d _ {q}}}\right).
$$

We can therefore apply the algebra of tensor product of spherical harmonics and use the machinery developed in [GMMM19b].

# C.3 Notations

Recall the definitions  $\pmb{d} = (d_{1},\dots ,d_{q})$ $\kappa = (\kappa_{1},\ldots ,\kappa_{Q})$ $d_q = d^{\eta_q}$ $r_q = d^{(\eta_q + \kappa_q) / 2}$ $D = d^{\eta_1} + \ldots +d^{\eta_Q}$  and  $R = (d^{\eta_1 + \kappa_1} + \ldots +d^{\eta_Q + \kappa_Q})^{1 / 2}$ . Let us denote  $\xi = \max_{q\in [Q]}\{\eta_q + \kappa_q\}$  and  $q_{\xi} = \arg \min_{q\in [Q]}\{\eta_q+$ $\kappa_q\}$ .

Recall that  $(\pmb{\theta}_i)_{i\in [N]}\sim \mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{D - 1}(\sqrt{D}))$  independently. Let  $\Theta = (\pmb {\theta}_1,\dots ,\pmb {\theta}_N)$ . We denote  $\mathbb{E}_{\pmb{\theta}}$  to be the expectation operator with respect to  $\pmb {\theta}\sim \mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{D - 1}(\sqrt{D}))$  and  $\mathbb{E}_{\Theta}$  the expectation operator with respect to  $\Theta = (\pmb {\theta}_1,\dots ,\pmb {\theta}_N)\sim \mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{D - 1}(\sqrt{D}))^{\otimes N}$ .

We will denote  $\mathbb{E}_{\overline{\pmb{\theta}}}$  the expectation operator with respect to  $\overline{\pmb{\theta}}\equiv (\overline{\pmb{\theta}}^{(1)},\dots ,\overline{\pmb{\theta}}^{(Q)})\sim \mu_{\pmb{d}},$ $\mathbb{E}_{\overline{\Theta}}$  the expectation operator with respect to  $\overline{\Theta} = (\overline{\theta}_1,\ldots ,\overline{\theta}_N)$ , and  $\mathbb{E}_{\pmb{\tau}}$  the expectation operator with respect to  $\pmb{\tau}$  (we recall  $\pmb {\tau}\equiv (\tau^{(1)},\dots,\tau^{(Q)})$ ) or  $(\pmb {\tau}_1,\dots,\pmb {\tau}_N)$  (where the  $\pmb {\tau}_i$  are independent) depending on the context. In particular, notice that  $\mathbb{E}_{\pmb{\theta}} = \mathbb{E}_{\pmb{\tau}}\mathbb{E}_{\overline{\pmb{\theta}}}$  and  $\mathbb{E}_{\Theta} = \mathbb{E}_{\tau}\mathbb{E}_{\overline{\Theta}}$

We will denote  $\mathbb{E}_{\Theta_{\varepsilon}}$  the expectation operator with respect to  $\Theta = (\theta_1, \dots, \theta_N)$  restricted to  $\mathcal{P}_{d,N,\varepsilon}$  and  $\mathbb{E}_{\pmb{\tau}_{\varepsilon}}$  the expectation operator with respect to  $\pmb{\tau}$  restricted to  $[1 - \varepsilon, 1 + \varepsilon]^Q$ . Notice that  $\mathbb{E}_{\Theta_{\varepsilon}} = \mathbb{E}_{\pmb{\tau}_{\varepsilon}} \mathbb{E}_{\overline{\Theta}}$ .

Let  $\mathbb{E}_{\pmb{x}}$  to be the expectation operator with respect to  $\pmb{x} \sim \mu_{\kappa}^{d}$ , and  $\mathbb{E}_{\overline{\pmb{x}}}$  the expectation operator with respect to  $\overline{\pmb{x}} \sim \mu_{\pmb{d}}$ .

# C.4 Generalization error of kernel ridge regression

We consider the Kernel Ridge Regression solution  $\hat{a}_i$ , namely

$$
\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} = (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {y},
$$

where the kernel matrix  $\pmb{H} = (H_{ij})_{ij\in [n]}$  is assumed to be given by

$$
H _ {i j} = \bar {h} _ {d} (\langle \pmb {x} _ {i}, \pmb {x} _ {j} \rangle / R ^ {2}) = \mathbb {E} _ {\pmb {\theta} \sim \mathrm {U n i f (P S} ^ {d})} [ \sigma (\langle \pmb {\overline {{\theta}}}, \pmb {x} \rangle / R) \sigma (\langle \pmb {\overline {{\theta}}}, \pmb {y} \rangle / R) ],
$$

and  $\pmb {y} = (y_{1},\dots ,y_{n})^{\mathsf{T}} = \pmb {f} + \pmb {\varepsilon}$  with

$$
\boldsymbol {f} = \left(f _ {d} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {1}), \dots , f _ {d} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {n})\right) ^ {\mathsf {T}},
$$

$$
\varepsilon = \left(\varepsilon_ {1}, \dots , \varepsilon_ {n}\right) ^ {\mathsf {T}}.
$$

The prediction function at location  $\pmb{x}$  gives

$$
\hat {f} _ {\lambda} (\boldsymbol {x}) = \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} (\boldsymbol {x}),
$$

with

$$
\pmb {h} (\pmb {x}) = [ \bar {h} _ {d} (\langle \pmb {x}, \pmb {x} _ {1} \rangle / R ^ {2}), \dots , \bar {h} _ {d} (\langle \pmb {x}, \pmb {x} _ {n} \rangle / R ^ {2}) ] ^ {\mathsf {T}}.
$$

The test error of empirical kernel ridge regression is defined as

$$
R _ {\mathrm {K R R}} (f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {X}, \lambda) \equiv \mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \Big [ \Big (f _ {d} (\boldsymbol {x}) - \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} (\boldsymbol {x}) \Big) ^ {2} \Big ].
$$

We define the set  $\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{KRR}}(\gamma) \subseteq \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^Q$  as follows (recall that  $\xi \equiv \max_{q \in [Q]} (\eta_q + \kappa_q)$ ):

$$
\bar {\mathcal {Q}} _ {\mathrm {K R R}} (\gamma) = \left\{\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q} \mid \sum_ {q = 1} ^ {Q} \left(\xi - \kappa_ {q}\right) k _ {q} \leq \gamma \right\}, \tag {41}
$$

and the function  $m:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}$  which at  $\gamma$  associates

$$
m(\gamma) = \min_{\boldsymbol {k}\notin \overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{KRR}}(\gamma)}\sum_{q\in [Q]}(\xi -\kappa_{q})k_{q}.
$$

Notice that by definition  $m(\gamma) > \gamma$

We consider sequences of problems indexed by the integer  $d$ , and we view the problem parameters (in particular, the dimensions  $d_q$ , the radii  $r_q$ , the kernel  $h_d$ , and so on) as functions of  $d$ .

Assumption 1. Let  $\{h_d\}_{d \geq 1}$  be a sequence of functions  $h_d: [-1,1] \to \mathbb{R}$  such that  $H_d(\pmb{x}_1, \pmb{x}_2) = h_d(\langle \pmb{x}_1, \pmb{x}_2 \rangle / d)$  is a positive semidefinite kernel.

(a) For  $\gamma > 0$  (which is specified in the theorem), we denote  $L = \max_{q \in [Q]} \lceil \gamma / \eta_q \rceil$ . We assume that  $h_d$  is  $L$ -weakly differentiable. We assume that for  $0 \leq k \leq L$ , the  $k$ -th weak derivative verifies almost surely  $h_d^{(k)}(u) \leq C$  for some constants  $C > 0$  independent of  $d$ . Furthermore, we assume there exists  $k > L$  such that  $h_d^{(k)}(0) \geq c > 0$  with  $c$  independent of  $d$ .  
(b) For  $\gamma > 0$  (which is specified in the theorem), we define

$$
\overline {{K}} = \max  _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \overline {{\mathcal {Q}}} _ {\mathrm {K R R}} (\gamma)} | \boldsymbol {k} |.
$$

We assume that  $\sigma$  verifies for  $k\leq \overline{K}$ ,  $h_d^{(k)}(0)\geq c$ , with  $c > 0$  independent of  $d$ .

Theorem 5 (Risk of the KRR model). Let  $\{f_d \in L^2(\mathrm{PS}_\kappa^d, \mu_d^\kappa)\}_{d \geq 1}$  be a sequence of functions. Assume  $w_d(d^\gamma \log d) \leq n \leq O_d(d^{m(\gamma) - \delta})$  for some  $\gamma > 0$  and  $\delta > 0$ . Let  $\{h_d\}_{d \geq 1}$  be a sequence of functions that satisfies Assumption 1 at level  $\gamma$ . Let  $\mathbf{X} = (\mathbf{x}_i)_{i \in [n]}$  with  $(\mathbf{x}_i)_{i \in [n]} \sim \mathrm{Unif}(\mathrm{PS}_\kappa^d)$  independently, and  $y_i = f_d(\mathbf{x}_i) + \varepsilon_i$  and  $\varepsilon_i \sim_{iid} \mathsf{N}(0, \tau^2)$  for some  $\tau^2 \geq 0$ . Then for any  $\varepsilon > 0$ , and for any  $\lambda = O_d(1)$ , with high probability we have

$$
\left| R _ {\mathrm {K R R}} \left(f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {X}, \lambda\right) - \left\| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \right\| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} \right| \leq \varepsilon \left(\left\| f _ {d} \right\| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} + \tau^ {2}\right). \tag {42}
$$

See Section D for the proof of this Theorem.

# C.5 Approximation error of the random features model

We consider the minimum population error for the random features model

$$
R _ {\mathrm {R F}} (f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}) = \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {F} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\boldsymbol {W})} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left(f _ {*} (\boldsymbol {x}) - f (\boldsymbol {x})\right) ^ {2} \right].
$$

Let us define the sets:

$$
\mathcal {Q} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\gamma) = \left\{\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q} \mid \sum_ {q = 1} ^ {Q} \left(\xi - \kappa_ {q}\right) k _ {q} <   \gamma \right\}, \tag {43}
$$

$$
\bar {Q} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\gamma) = \left\{\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q} \mid \sum_ {q = 1} ^ {Q} \left(\xi - \kappa_ {q}\right) k _ {q} \leq \gamma \right\}. \tag {44}
$$

Assumption 2. Let  $\sigma$  be an activation function.

(a) There exists constants  $c_0, c_1$ , with  $c_0 > 0$  and  $c_1 < 1$  such that the activation function  $\sigma$  verifies  $\sigma(u)^2 \leq c_0 \exp(c_1 u^2 / 2)$  almost surely for  $u \in \mathbb{R}$ .  
(b) For  $\gamma > 0$  (which is specified in the theorem), we denote  $L = \max_{q \in [Q]} \lceil \gamma / \eta_q \rceil$ . We assume that  $\sigma$  is  $L$ -weakly differentiable. Define

$$
K = \min  _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\gamma) ^ {c}} | \boldsymbol {k} |.
$$

We assume that for  $K \leq k \leq L$ , the  $k$ -th weak derivative verifies almost surely  $\sigma^{(k)}(u)^2 \leq c_0 \exp(c_1 u^2 / 2)$  for some constants  $c_0 > 0$  and  $c_1 < 1$ .

Furthermore we will assume that  $\sigma$  is not a degree- $\lfloor \gamma / \eta_{q_{\xi}} \rfloor$  polynomial where we recall that  $q_{\xi}$  corresponds to the unique  $\arg \min_{q \in [Q]} \{\eta_q + \kappa_q\}$ .

(c) For  $\gamma > 0$  (which is specified in the theorem), we define

$$
\overline {{K}} = \max  _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \overline {{Q}} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\gamma)} | \boldsymbol {k} |.
$$

We assume that  $\sigma$  verifies for  $k\leq \overline{K}$ $\mu_k(\sigma)\neq 0$  . Furthermore we assume that for  $k\leq \overline{K}$  , the  $k$  -th weak derivative verifies almost surely  $\sigma^{(k)}(u)^2\leq c_0\exp (c_1u^2 /2)$  for some constants  $c_{0} > 0$  and  $c_{1} <   1$

Assumption 2.(a) implies that  $\sigma \in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\gamma)$  where  $\gamma (\mathrm{d}x) = e^{-x^2 /2}\mathrm{d}x / \sqrt{2\pi}$  is the standard Gaussian measure. We recall the Hermite decomposition of  $\sigma$

$$
\sigma (x) = \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\infty} \frac {\mu_ {k} (\sigma)}{k !} \mathrm {H e} _ {k} (x), \quad \mu_ {k} (\sigma) \equiv \mathbb {E} _ {G \sim \mathrm {N} (0, 1)} [ \sigma (G) \mathrm {H e} _ {k} (G) ]. \tag {45}
$$

Theorem 6 (Risk of the RF model). Let  $\{f_d \in L^2(\mathrm{PS}_\kappa^d, \mu_\pmb{d}^\kappa)\}_{d \geq 1}$  be a sequence of functions. Let  $\pmb{W} = (\pmb{w}_i)_{i \in [N]}$  with  $(\pmb{w}_i)_{i \in [N]} \sim \mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{D-1})$  independently. We have the following results.

(a) Assume  $N \leq o_d(d^\gamma)$  for a fixed  $\gamma > 0$ . Let  $\sigma$  satisfy Assumptions 2.(a) and 2.(b) at level  $\gamma$ . Then, for any  $\varepsilon > 0$ , the following holds with high probability:

$$
\left| R _ {\mathrm {R F}} \left(f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}\right) - R _ {\mathrm {R F}} \left(\mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}\right) - \| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} \right| \leq \varepsilon \| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} \| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}}, \tag {46}
$$

where  $\mathcal{Q} \equiv \mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{RF}}(\gamma)$  is defined in Equation (43).

(b) Assume  $N \geq w_{d}(d^{\gamma})$  for some positive constant  $\gamma > 0$ , and  $\sigma$  satisfy Assumptions 2. (a) and 2. (c) at level  $\gamma$ . Then for any  $\varepsilon > 0$ , the following holds with high probability:

$$
0 \leq R _ {\mathrm {R F}} \left(\mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}\right) \leq \varepsilon \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}, \tag {47}
$$

where  $\mathcal{Q}\equiv \overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{RF}}(\gamma)$  is defined in Equation (44).

See Section E for the proof of the lower bound (46), and Section F for the proof of the upper bound (47).

Remark 1. This theorems show that for each  $\gamma \notin (\xi -\kappa_1)\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} + \ldots +(\xi -\kappa_Q)\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$ , we can decompose our functional space as

$$
L ^ {2} \left(\mathrm {P S} _ {\kappa} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}}, \mu_ {\boldsymbol {d}} ^ {\kappa}\right) = \mathcal {F} (\boldsymbol {\beta}, \kappa , \gamma) \oplus \mathcal {F} ^ {c} (\boldsymbol {\beta}, \kappa , \gamma),
$$

where

$$
\mathcal {F} (\boldsymbol {\beta}, \kappa , \gamma) = \bigoplus_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\gamma)} V _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}},
$$

$$
\mathcal {F} ^ {c} (\boldsymbol {\beta}, \boldsymbol {\kappa}, \gamma) = \bigoplus_ {\boldsymbol {k} \notin \mathcal {Q} _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\gamma)} \boldsymbol {V} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}},
$$

such that for  $N = d^{\gamma}$ , RF model fits the subspace of low degree polynomials  $\mathcal{F}(\beta, \kappa, \gamma)$  and cannot fit  $\mathcal{F}^{c}(\beta, \kappa, \gamma)$ , i.e.

$$
R _ {\mathrm {R F}} (f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}) \approx \| \mathsf {P} _ {Q _ {\mathrm {R F}} (\gamma) ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}.
$$

Remark 2. In other words, we can fit a polynomial of degree  $\pmb{k} \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}$ , if and only if

$$
d ^ {(\xi - \kappa_ {1}) k _ {1}} \cdot \ldots \cdot d ^ {(\xi - \kappa_ {Q}) k _ {Q}} = d _ {1, \mathrm {e f f}} ^ {k _ {1}} \ldots d _ {Q, \mathrm {e f f}} ^ {k _ {Q}} = o _ {d} (N).
$$

Each subspace has therefore an effective dimension  $d_{q,\mathrm{eff}} \equiv d^{\xi - \kappa_q} = d_q^{(\xi - \kappa_q) / \eta_q} \asymp D^{(\xi - \kappa_q) / \max_{q \in [Q]} \eta_q}$ . This can be understood intuitively as follows,

$$
\sigma \left(\langle \boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {x} \rangle / R\right) = \sigma \left(\sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} \langle \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {(q)}, \boldsymbol {x} ^ {(q)} \rangle / R\right).
$$

The term  $q_{\xi}$  (recall that  $q_{\xi} = \arg \max_q(\eta_q + \kappa_q)$  and  $\xi = \eta_{q_{\xi}} + \kappa_{q_{\xi}}$ ) verifies  $\langle \pmb{\theta}^{(q_{\xi})}, \pmb{x}^{(q_{\xi})} \rangle / R = \Theta_d(1)$  and has the same effective dimension  $d_{q_{\xi},\mathrm{eff}} = d^{\eta_{q_{\xi}}}$  has in the uniform case restricted to the sphere  $\mathbb{S}^{d^{\eta_q} - 1}(\sqrt{d^{\eta_q}})$  (the scaling of the sphere does not matter because of the global normalization factor  $R^{-1}$ ). However, for  $\eta_q + \kappa_q < \xi$ , we have  $\langle \pmb{\theta}^{(q)}, \pmb{x}^{(q)} \rangle / R = \Theta_d(d^{(\eta_q + \kappa_q - \xi) / 2})$  and we will need  $d^{\xi - \kappa_q - \eta_q}$  more neurons to capture the dependency on the  $q$ -th sphere coordinates. The effective dimension is therefore given by  $d_{q,\mathrm{eff}} = d_q \cdot d^{\xi - \kappa_q - \eta_q} = d^{\xi - \kappa_q}$ .

# C.6 Approximation error of the neural tangent model

We consider the minimum population error for the random features model

$$
R _ {\mathrm {N T}} (f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}) = \inf  _ {f \in \mathcal {F} _ {\mathrm {N T}} (\boldsymbol {W})} \mathbb {E} \big [ (f _ {*} (\boldsymbol {x}) - f (\boldsymbol {x})) ^ {2} \big ].
$$

For  $\pmb{k} \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}$ , we denote by  $S(\pmb{k}) \subseteq [Q]$  the subset of indices  $q \in [Q]$  such that  $k_{q} > 0$ .

We define the sets

$$
\mathcal {Q} _ {\mathrm {N T}} (\gamma) = \left\{\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q} \mid \sum_ {q = 1} ^ {Q} \left(\xi - \kappa_ {q}\right) k _ {q} <   \gamma + \left(\xi - \min  _ {q \in S (\boldsymbol {k})} \kappa_ {q}\right) \right\}, \tag {48}
$$

$$
\overline {{\mathcal {Q}}} _ {\mathrm {N T}} (\gamma) = \left\{\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q} \mid \sum_ {q = 1} ^ {Q} \left(\xi - \kappa_ {q}\right) k _ {q} \leq \gamma + \left(\xi - \min  _ {q \in S (\boldsymbol {k})} \kappa_ {q}\right) \right\}. \tag {49}
$$

Assumption 3. Let  $\sigma : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$  be an activation function.

(a) The activation function  $\sigma$  is weakly differentiable with weak derivative  $\sigma'$ . There exists constants  $c_0, c_1$ , with  $c_0 > 0$  and  $c_1 < 1$  such that the activation function  $\sigma$  verifies  $\sigma'(u)^2 \leq c_0 \exp(c_1 u^2 / 2)$  almost surely for  $u \in \mathbb{R}$ .  
(b) For  $\gamma > 0$  (which is specified in the theorem), we denote  $L = \max_{q \in [Q]} \lceil \gamma / \eta_q \rceil$ . We assume that  $\sigma'$  is  $L$ -weakly differentiable. Define

$$
K = \min  _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} _ {\mathrm {N T}} (\gamma) ^ {c}} | \boldsymbol {k} |.
$$

We assume that for  $K - 1 \leq k \leq L$ , the  $k$ -th weak derivative verifies almost surely  $\sigma^{(k + 1)}(u)^2 \leq c_0 \exp(c_1 u^2 / 2)$  for some constants  $c_0 > 0$  and  $c_1 < 1$ .

Furthermore, we assume that  $\sigma'$  verifies a non-degeneracy condition. Recall that  $\mu_k(h) \equiv \mathbb{E}_{G \sim \mathsf{N}(0,1)}[h(G)\mathrm{He}_k(G)]$  denote the  $k$ -th coefficient of the Hermite expansion of  $h \in L_2(\mathbb{R},\gamma)$  (with  $\gamma$  the standard Gaussian measure). Then there exists  $k_1, k_2 \geq 2L + 7[\max_{q \in [Q]} \xi/\eta_q]$  such that  $\mu_{k_1}(\sigma'), \mu_{k_2}(\sigma') \neq 0$  and

$$
\frac {\mu_ {k _ {1}} \left(x ^ {2} \sigma^ {\prime}\right)}{\mu_ {k _ {1}} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right)} \neq \frac {\mu_ {k _ {2}} \left(x ^ {2} \sigma^ {\prime}\right)}{\mu_ {k _ {2}} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right)}. \tag {50}
$$

(c) For  $\gamma > 0$  (which is specified in the theorem), we define

$$
\overline {{K}} = \max  _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \overline {{Q}} _ {\mathrm {N T}} (\gamma)} | \boldsymbol {k} |.
$$

We assume that  $\sigma$  verifies for  $k\leq \overline{K} +1$ ,  $\mu_k(\sigma ') = \mu_{k + 1}(\sigma)\neq 0$ . Furthermore we assume that for  $k\leq \overline{K} +1$ , the  $k$ -th weak derivative verifies almost surely  $\sigma^{(k + 1)}(u)^2\leq c_0\exp (c_1u^2 /2)$  for some constants  $c_{0} > 0$  and  $c_{1} < 1$ .

Assumption 3.(a) implies that  $\sigma' \in L^2(\mathbb{R},\gamma)$  where  $\gamma(\mathrm{d}x) = e^{-x^2/2}\mathrm{d}x/\sqrt{2\pi}$  is the standard Gaussian measure. We recall the Hermite decomposition of  $\sigma'$ :

$$
\sigma^ {\prime} (x) = \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\infty} \frac {\mu_ {k} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right)}{k !} \operatorname {H e} _ {k} (x), \quad \mu_ {k} \left(\sigma^ {\prime}\right) \equiv \mathbb {E} _ {G \sim \mathrm {N} (0, 1)} \left[ \sigma^ {\prime} (G) \operatorname {H e} _ {k} (G) \right]. \tag {51}
$$

In the Assumption 3.(b), it is useful to notice that the Hermite coefficients of  $x^{2}\sigma^{\prime}(x)$  can be computed from the ones of  $\sigma^{\prime}(x)$  using the relation  $\mu_{k}(x^{2}\sigma^{\prime}) = \mu_{k + 2}(\sigma^{\prime}) + [1 + 2k]\mu_{k}(\sigma^{\prime}) + k(k - 1)\mu_{k - 2}(\sigma^{\prime})$ .

Theorem 7 (Risk of the NT model). Let  $\{f_d \in L^2(\mathrm{PS}_\kappa^d, \mu_{\pmb{d}}^\kappa)\}_{d \geq 1}$  be a sequence of functions. Let  $\pmb{W} = (\pmb{w}_i)_{i \in [N]}$  with  $(\pmb{w}_i)_{i \in [N]} \sim \mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{D-1})$  independently. We have the following results.

(a) Assume  $N \leq o_d(d^\gamma)$  for a fixed  $\gamma > 0$ . Let  $\sigma$  satisfy Assumptions 3.(a) and 3.(b) at level  $\gamma$ . Then, for any  $\varepsilon > 0$ , the following holds with high probability:

$$
\left| R _ {\mathrm {N T}} \left(f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}\right) - R _ {\mathrm {N T}} \left(\mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}\right) - \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} \right| \leq \varepsilon \| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}}, \tag {52}
$$

where  $\mathcal{Q}\equiv \mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{NT}}(\gamma)$  is defined in Equation (48).

(b) Assume  $N \geq w_{d}(d^{\gamma})$  for some positive constant  $\gamma > 0$ , and  $\sigma$  satisfy Assumptions 3.(a) and 3.(c) at level  $\gamma$ . Then for any  $\varepsilon > 0$ , the following holds with high probability:

$$
0 \leq R _ {\mathrm {N T}} \left(\mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}\right) \leq \varepsilon \| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}, \tag {53}
$$

where  $\mathcal{Q}\equiv \overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{NT}}(\gamma)$  is defined in Equation (49).

See Section G for the proof of lower bound, and Section H for the proof of upper bound.

Remark 3. This theorems show that each for each  $\gamma >0$  such that  $\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{NT}}(\gamma)^c\cap \overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{NT}}(\gamma) = \emptyset$ , we can decompose our functional space as

$$
L ^ {2} \left(\mathrm {P S} _ {\kappa} ^ {d}, \mu_ {d} ^ {\kappa}\right) = \mathcal {F} (\beta , \kappa , \gamma) \oplus \mathcal {F} ^ {c} (\beta , \kappa , \gamma),
$$

where

$$
\mathcal {F} (\beta , \kappa , \gamma) = \bigoplus_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} _ {\mathrm {N T}} (\gamma)} \boldsymbol {V} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {d},
$$

$$
\mathcal{F}^{c}(\boldsymbol {\beta},\boldsymbol {\kappa},\gamma) = \bigoplus_{\boldsymbol {k}\not\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{NT}}(\gamma)}\boldsymbol{V}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{d}},
$$

such that for  $N = d^{\gamma}$ , NT model fits the subspace of low degree polynomials  $\mathcal{F}(\beta, \kappa, \gamma)$  and cannot fit  $\mathcal{F}^c(\beta, \kappa, \gamma)$  at all, i.e.

$$
R _ {\mathrm {N T}} (f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {W}) \approx \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} _ {\mathrm {N T}} (\gamma) ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}.
$$

Remark 4. In other words, we can fit a polynomial of degree  $\pmb{k} \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}$ , if and only if

$$
d ^ {(\xi - \kappa_ {1}) k _ {1}} \cdot \ldots \cdot d ^ {(\xi - \kappa_ {Q}) k _ {Q}} = d _ {1, \mathrm {e f f}} ^ {k _ {1}} \cdot \ldots d _ {Q, \mathrm {e f f}} ^ {k _ {Q}} = o _ {d} (d ^ {\beta} N),
$$

where  $\beta = \xi -\min_{q\in S(\pmb {k})}\kappa_q$

# C.7 Connecting to the theorems in the main text

Let us connect the above general results to the two-spheres setting described in the main text. We consider two spheres with  $\eta_1 = \eta$ ,  $\kappa_1 = \kappa$  for the first sphere, and  $\eta_2 = 1$ ,  $\kappa_2 = 0$  for the second sphere. We have  $\xi = \max(\eta + \kappa, 1)$ .

Let  $w_{d}(d^{\gamma}\log d)\leq n\leq O_{d}(d^{\gamma +\delta})$  with  $\delta >0$  constant sufficiently small, then by Theorem 5 the function subspace learned by KRR is given by the polynomials of degree  $k_{1}$  in the first sphere coordinates and  $k_{2}$  in the second sphere with

$$
\max (\eta , 1 - \kappa) k _ {1} + \max (\eta + \kappa , 1) k _ {2} <   \gamma .
$$

We consider functions that only depend on the first sphere, i.e.,  $k_{2} = 0$  and denote  $d_{\mathrm{eff}} = d^{\max(\eta,1 - \kappa)}$ . Then the subspace of approximation is given by the  $k$  polynomials in the first sphere such that  $d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{k} \leq d^{\gamma}$ . Furthermore, one can check that the Assumptions listed in Theorem 1 in the main text verifies Assumption 1.

Similarly, for  $w_{d}(d^{\gamma}) \leq N \leq O_{d}(d^{\gamma + \delta})$  with  $\delta > 0$  constant sufficiently small, Theorem 6 implies that the RF models can only approximate  $k$  polynomials in the first sphere such that  $d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{k} \leq d^{\gamma}$ . Furthermore, Assumptions listed in Theorem 2 in the main text verifies Assumption 2.

In the case of NT, we only consider  $\pmb{k} = (k_{1},0)$  and  $S(\pmb{k}) = \{1\}$ . We get  $\min_{q \in S(\pmb{k})} \kappa_q = \kappa$ . The subspace approximated is given by the  $k$  polynomials in the first sphere such that  $d_{\mathrm{eff}}^k \leq d^\gamma d_{\mathrm{eff}}$ . Furthermore, Assumptions listed in Theorem 3 in the main text verifies Assumption 3.

# D Proof of Theorem 5

The proof follows closely the proof of [GMMM19b, Theorem 4].

# D.1 Preliminaries

Let us rewrite the kernel functions  $\{h_d\}_{d\geq 1}$  as functions on the product of normalized spheres: for  $\pmb{x} = \{\pmb{x}^{(q)}\}_{q\in [Q]}$  and  $\pmb{y} = \{\pmb{y}^{(q)}\}_{q\in [Q]}\in \mathrm{PS}_{\kappa}^{\overline{\pmb{d}}}$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} h _ {d} \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {y}, \boldsymbol {x} \right\rangle / R ^ {2}\right) = h _ {d} \left(\sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} \left(r _ {q} ^ {2} / R ^ {2} \sqrt {d _ {q}}\right) \cdot \left\langle \overline {{\boldsymbol {y}}} ^ {(q)}, \overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} ^ {(q)} \right\rangle / \sqrt {d _ {q}}\right) \tag {54} \\ \equiv h _ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(\{\langle \overline {{\boldsymbol {y}}} ^ {(q)}, \overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} ^ {(q)} \rangle / \sqrt {d _ {q}} \} _ {q \in [ Q ]}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

Consider the expansion of  $h_d$  in terms of tensor product of Gegenbauer polynomials. We have

$$
h_{d}(\langle \boldsymbol {y},\boldsymbol {x}\rangle /R^{2}) = \sum_{\boldsymbol {k}\in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{d}}(h_{\boldsymbol{d}})B(\boldsymbol {d},\boldsymbol {k})Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{d}}\left(\{\langle \overline{\boldsymbol{y}}^{(q)},\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(q)}\rangle \}_{q\in [Q]}\right),
$$

where

$$
\lambda_ {k} ^ {d} (h _ {d}) = \mathbb {E} _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}}} \Big [ h _ {d} \left(\overline {{x}} _ {1} ^ {(1)}, \ldots , \overline {{x}} _ {1} ^ {(Q)}\right) Q _ {k} ^ {d} \Big (\sqrt {d _ {1}} \overline {{x}} _ {1} ^ {(1)}, \ldots , \sqrt {d _ {Q}} \overline {{x}} _ {1} ^ {(Q)} \Big) \Big ],
$$

where the expectation is taken over  $\overline{\pmb{x}} = (\overline{\pmb{x}}^{(1)},\dots ,\overline{\pmb{x}}^{(Q)})\sim \mu_{\pmb{d}}$

Lemma 2. Let  $\{h_d\}_{d\geq 1}$  be a sequence of kernel functions that satisfies Assumption 1. Assume  $w_{d}(d^{\gamma})\leq n\leq o_{d}(d^{m(\gamma)})$  for some  $\gamma >0$ . Consider  $\mathcal{Q} = \overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathrm{RR}}(\gamma)$  as defined in Eq. (41). Then there exists constants  $c,C > 0$  such that for  $d$  large enough,

$$
\max _ {\boldsymbol {k} \not \in \mathcal {Q}} \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) \leq C d ^ {- m (\gamma)},
$$

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q}} \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) \geq c d ^ {- \gamma}.
$$

Proof of Lemma 2. Notice that by Lemma 18,

$$
\lambda_ {\pmb {k}} ^ {\pmb {d}} (h _ {\pmb {d}}) = \left(\prod_ {q \in [ Q ]} \alpha_ {q} ^ {k _ {q}}\right) \cdot R (\pmb {d}, \pmb {k}) \cdot \mathbb {E} _ {\overline {{\pmb {x}}}} \left[ \left(\prod_ {q \in [ Q ]} \left(1 - \frac {(\overline {{x}} _ {1} ^ {(q)}) ^ {2}}{d _ {q}}\right) ^ {k _ {q}}\right) \cdot h _ {d} ^ {(| \pmb {k} |)} \left(\sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} \alpha_ {q} \overline {{x}} _ {1} ^ {(q)}\right) \right],
$$

where  $\alpha_{q} = d_{q}^{-1 / 2}r_{q}^{2} / R^{2} = (1 + o_{d}(1))d^{\eta_{q} / 2 + \kappa_{q} - \xi}$ . By Assumption 1.(a), we have

$$
\lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k}) \leq C \prod_ {q \in [ Q ]} d ^ {(\kappa_ {q} - \xi) k _ {q}}.
$$

Furthermore, by Assumption 1.(b) and dominated convergence,

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}}} \left[ \left(\prod_ {q \in [ Q ]} \left(1 - \frac {(\overline {{x}} _ {1} ^ {(q)}) ^ {2}}{d _ {q}}\right) ^ {k _ {q}}\right) \cdot h _ {d} ^ {(| \boldsymbol {k} |)} \left(\sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} \alpha_ {q} \overline {{x}} _ {1} ^ {(q)}\right) \right] \to h _ {d} ^ {(| \boldsymbol {k} |)} (0) \geq c > 0,
$$

for  $k \geq \overline{K}$ . The lemma then follows from the same proof as in Lemma 9 and Lemma 10, where we adapt the proofs of Lemma 19 and 20 to  $h_d$ .

# D.2 Proof of Theorem 5

Step 1. Rewrite the  $y, E, H, M$  matrices.

The test error of empirical kernel ridge regression gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} R _ {\mathrm {K R R}} \left(f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {X}, \lambda\right) \equiv \mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ \left(f _ {d} (\boldsymbol {x}) - \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {h} (\boldsymbol {x})\right)\right) ^ {2} \Bigg ] \\ = \mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} [ f _ {d} (\boldsymbol {x}) ^ {2} ] - 2 \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\mathsf {T}} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {E} + \boldsymbol {y} ^ {\mathsf {T}} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {y}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\pmb {E} = (E_1,\dots ,E_n)^\top$  and  $M = (M_{ij})_{ij\in [n]}$ , with

$$
E _ {i} = \mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ f _ {d} (\boldsymbol {x}) h _ {d} \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {x} _ {i} \right\rangle / d\right) \right],
$$

$$
M _ {i j} = \mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {x}} \left[ h _ {d} \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {x} \right\rangle / d\right) h _ {d} \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {j}, \boldsymbol {x} \right\rangle / d\right) \right].
$$

Let  $B = \sum_{\pmb{k} \in \mathcal{Q}} B(\pmb{d}, \pmb{k})$ . Define for any  $\pmb{k} \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}$ ,

$$
\boldsymbol {D} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} = \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}\right) \mathbf {I} _ {B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k})},
$$

$$
\pmb {Y} _ {\pmb {k}} = (Y _ {\pmb {k}, \pmb {s}} ^ {\pmb {d}} (\overline {{\pmb {x}}} _ {i})) _ {i \in [ n ], \pmb {s} \in [ B (\pmb {d}, \pmb {k}) ]} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times B (\pmb {d}, \pmb {k})},
$$

$$
\pmb {\lambda} _ {\pmb {k}} = (\lambda_ {\pmb {k}, \pmb {s}} ^ {\pmb {d}} (f _ {d})) _ {\pmb {s} \in [ B (\pmb {d}, \pmb {k}) ]} ^ {\top} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {B (\pmb {d}, \pmb {k})},
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} = \mathrm {d i a g} \left(\left(\lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) \mathbf {I} _ {B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k})}\right) _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q}}\right) \in \mathbb {R} ^ {B \times B}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} = (\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}}) _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q}} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times B},
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} = \left(\left(\boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\mathsf {T}}\right) _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q}}\right) ^ {\mathsf {T}} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {B}.
$$

Let the spherical harmonics decomposition of  $f_{d}$  be

$$
f_{d}(\boldsymbol {x}) = \sum_{\boldsymbol {k}\in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}}\sum_{\boldsymbol {s}\in [B(\boldsymbol {d},\boldsymbol {k})]} \lambda_{\boldsymbol {k},\boldsymbol{s}}^{d}(f_{d})Y_{\boldsymbol {k},\boldsymbol{s}}^{d}(\overline{\boldsymbol{x}}),
$$

and the Gegenbauer decomposition of  $h_d$  be

$$
h_{\boldsymbol{d}}\left(\overline{x}_{1}^{(1)},\ldots ,\overline{x}_{1}^{(Q)}\right) = \sum_{\boldsymbol {k}\in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{d}}(h_{\boldsymbol{d}})B(\boldsymbol {d},\boldsymbol {k})Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{d}}\left(\sqrt{d_{1}}\overline{x}_{1}^{(1)},\ldots ,\sqrt{d_{Q}}\overline{x}_{1}^{(Q)}\right).
$$

We write the decompositions of vectors  $\pmb{f}$ ,  $\pmb{E}$ ,  $\pmb{H}$ , and  $\pmb{M}$ . We have

$$
\boldsymbol {f} = Y _ {\mathcal {Q}} \lambda_ {\mathcal {Q}} + \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} Y _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}},
$$

$$
E = Y _ {\mathcal {Q}} D _ {\mathcal {Q}} \lambda_ {\mathcal {Q}} + \sum_ {k \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} Y _ {k} D _ {k} \lambda_ {k},
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {H} = \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} + \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top},
$$

$$
M = Y _ {\mathcal {Q}} D _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {2} Y _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} + \sum_ {k \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} Y _ {k} D _ {k} ^ {2} Y _ {k} ^ {\top}.
$$

From Lemma 4, we can rewrite

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {H} = \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} + \kappa_ {h} (\mathbf {I} _ {n} + \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h}), \\ \boldsymbol {M} = \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} + \kappa_ {u} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {u}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\kappa_{h} = \Theta_{d}(1)$ ,  $\kappa_{u} = O_{d}(d^{-m(\gamma)})$ ,  $\| \pmb{\Delta}_{h}\|_{\mathrm{op}} = o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$  and  $\| \pmb{\Delta}_{u}\|_{\mathrm{op}} = O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$ .

# Step 2. Decompose the risk

The rest of the proof follows closely from [GMMM19b, Theorem 4]. We decompose the risk as follows

$$
R _ {\mathrm {K R R}} \left(f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {X}, \lambda\right) = \left\| f _ {d} \right\| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} - 2 T _ {1} + T _ {2} + T _ {3} - 2 T _ {4} + 2 T _ {5}.
$$

where

$$
\begin{array}{l} T _ {1} = \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {E}, \\ T _ {2} = \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}, \\ T _ {3} = \varepsilon^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \varepsilon , \\ T _ {4} = \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\mathsf {T}} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {E}, \\ T _ {5} = \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\mathsf {T}} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Further, we denote  $f_{\mathcal{Q}}, f_{\mathcal{Q}^c}, E_{\mathcal{Q}}$ , and  $E_{\mathcal{Q}^c}$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}} = Y _ {\mathcal {Q}} \lambda_ {\mathcal {Q}}, \quad \boldsymbol {E} _ {\mathcal {Q}} = Y _ {\mathcal {Q}} D _ {\mathcal {Q}} \lambda_ {\mathcal {Q}}, \\ f _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} = \sum_ {k \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} Y _ {k} \lambda_ {k}, \quad E _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} = \sum_ {k \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} Y _ {k} D _ {k} \lambda_ {k}. \\ \end{array}
$$

# Step 3. Term  $T_{2}$

Note we have

$$
T _ {2} = T _ {2 1} + T _ {2 2} + T _ {2 3},
$$

where

$$
\begin{array}{l} T _ {2 1} = \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}}, \\ T _ {2 2} = 2 \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathrm {T}} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {\circ}}, \\ T _ {2 3} = \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

By Lemma 6, we have

$$
\left\| n \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} - \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathrm {T}} / n \right\| _ {\text {o p}} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1), \tag {55}
$$

hence

$$
\begin{array}{l} T _ {2 1} = \lambda_ {\mathcal {Q}} \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {M} (\mathbf {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \lambda_ {\mathcal {Q}} \\ = \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} / n ^ {2} + \left[ \| \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \| _ {2} ^ {2} / n \right] \cdot o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1). \\ \end{array}
$$

By Lemma 3, we have (with  $\| \Delta \|_2 = o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$ )

$$
\lambda_ {Q} ^ {\top} \mathbf {Y} _ {Q} ^ {\top} \mathbf {Y} _ {Q} \mathbf {Y} _ {Q} ^ {\top} \mathbf {Y} _ {Q} \lambda_ {Q} / n ^ {2} = \lambda_ {Q} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {I} _ {B} + \boldsymbol {\Delta}) ^ {2} \lambda_ {Q} = \| \lambda_ {Q} \| _ {2} ^ {2} (1 + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1)).
$$

Moreover, we have

$$
\left\| \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} / n = \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {I} _ {B} + \boldsymbol {\Delta}) \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} = \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} (1 + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1)).
$$

As a result, we have

$$
T _ {2 1} = \left\| \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \left(1 + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1)\right) = \left\| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \right\| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} \left(1 + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1)\right). \tag {56}
$$

By Eq. (55) again, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} T _ {2 3} = \left(\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top}\right) (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \boldsymbol {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \boldsymbol {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \left(\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}}\right) \\ = \Big (\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} \Big) \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} \Big (\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \Big) / n ^ {2} + \left[ \left\| \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} / n \right] \cdot o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1). \\ \end{array}
$$

By Lemma 5, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left(\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top}\right) \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} \left(\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}}\right) \right] / n ^ {2} = \sum_ {\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {v} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {u}} ^ {\top} \{\mathbb {E} [ (\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {u}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {v}}) ] / n ^ {2} \} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {v}} \\ = \frac {B}{n} \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \| \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Moreover

$$
\mathbb {E} \Big [ \Big \| \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \Big \| _ {2} ^ {2} / n \Big ] = \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}.
$$

This gives

$$
T _ {2 3} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}. \tag {57}
$$

Using Cauchy Schwarz inequality for  $T_{22}$ , we get

$$
T _ {2 2} \leq 2 \left(T _ {2 1} T _ {2 3}\right) ^ {1 / 2} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} \| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}}. \tag {58}
$$

As a result, combining Eqs. (56), (58) and (57), we have

$$
T _ {2} = \left\| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \right\| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \left\| f _ {d} \right\| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}. \tag {59}
$$

Step 4. Term  $T_{1}$ . Note we have

$$
T _ {1} = T _ {1 1} + T _ {1 2} + T _ {1 3},
$$

where

$$
T _ {1 1} = \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\intercal} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {E} _ {\mathcal {Q}},
$$

$$
T _ {1 2} = \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {E} _ {\mathcal {Q}},
$$

$$
T _ {1 3} = \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\mathsf {T}} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {E} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}}.
$$

By Lemma 7, we have

$$
\left\| \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} \left(\mathbf {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \mathbf {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} - \mathbf {I} _ {B} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1).
$$

so that

$$
T _ {1 1} = \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} = \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \| _ {2} ^ {2} (1 + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1)) = \| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \| _ {2} ^ {2} (1 + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1)). \tag {60}
$$

Using Cauchy Schwarz inequality for  $T_{12}$ , and by the expression of  $\pmb{M} = \pmb{Y}_{\mathcal{Q}}\pmb{D}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\pmb{Y}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top} + \kappa_{u}\pmb{\Delta}_{u}$  with  $\| \pmb{\Delta}_u\|_{\mathrm{op}} = O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$  and  $\kappa_{u} = O_{d}(d^{-m(\lambda)})$ , we get with high probability

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| T _ {1 2} \right| = \left| \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\intercal} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \boldsymbol {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \right| \\ \leq \Big \| \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \boldsymbol {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \Big \| _ {2} \| \lambda_ {\mathcal {Q}} \| _ {2} \\ = \left[ \left(\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\mathrm {T}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\mathrm {T}}\right) (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathrm {T}} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \left(\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\mathrm {T}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\mathrm {T}}\right) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \| _ {2} \tag {61} \\ \leq \left[ \left(\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\mathrm {T}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\mathrm {T}}\right) (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \boldsymbol {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \boldsymbol {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \left(\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\mathrm {T}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\mathrm {T}}\right) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \| _ {2} \\ = T _ {2 3} ^ {1 / 2} \| \lambda_ {\mathcal {Q}} \| _ {2} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

For term  $T_{13}$ , we have

$$
\left| T _ {1 3} \right| = \left| \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\mathsf {T}} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {E} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \right| \leq \| \boldsymbol {f} \| _ {2} \| \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| \boldsymbol {E} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \| _ {2}.
$$

Note we have  $\mathbb{E}[\| \pmb {f}\| _2^2 ] = n\| f_d\|_{L^2}^2$  , and  $\| (\pmb {H} + \lambda \mathbf{I}_n)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2 / (\kappa_h + \lambda)$  with high probability, and

$$
\mathbb {E} [ \| \boldsymbol {E} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \| _ {2} ^ {2} ] = n \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) ^ {2} \| \mathsf {P} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} \leq n \left[ \max  _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) ^ {2} \right] \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}.
$$

As a result, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| T _ {1 3} \right| \leq O _ {d} (1) \cdot \left\| \mathbb {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \right\| _ {L ^ {2}} \| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} \left[ n ^ {2} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}\right) ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2} / (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) \tag {62} \\ = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} \| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last equality used the fact that  $n \leq O_d(d^{m(\gamma) - \delta})$  and Lemma 2. Combining Eqs. (60), (61) and (62), we get

$$
T _ {1} = \left\| P _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \right\| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \left\| f _ {d} \right\| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}. \tag {63}
$$

Step 5. Terms  $T_{3}, T_{4}$  and  $T_{5}$ . By Lemma 6 again, we have

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {\varepsilon}} [ T _ {3} ] / \tau^ {2} = \mathrm {t r} ((\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1}) = \mathrm {t r} (\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\intercal} / n ^ {2}) + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1),
$$

By Lemma 3, we have

$$
\mathrm {t r} (\pmb {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \pmb {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} / n ^ {2}) = \mathrm {t r} (\pmb {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} \pmb {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}}) / n ^ {2} = n B / n ^ {2} + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1).
$$

This gives

$$
T _ {3} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \tau^ {2}. \tag {64}
$$

Let us consider  $T_4$  term:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {\varepsilon}} \left[ T _ {4} ^ {2} \right] / \tau^ {2} = \mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {\varepsilon}} \left[ \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {E} \boldsymbol {E} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \right] / \tau^ {2} \\ = \boldsymbol {E} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 2} \boldsymbol {E}. \\ \end{array}
$$

For any integer  $L$ , denote  $\mathcal{L} \equiv [0, L]^Q \cap \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^Q$ , and  $\mathbf{Y}_{\mathcal{L}} = (\mathbf{Y}_k)_{k \in \mathcal{L}}$  and  $\mathbf{D}_{\mathcal{L}} = (\mathbf{D}_k)_{k \in \mathcal{L}}$ . Then notice that by Lemma 3, Lemma 6 and the definition of  $\mathbf{M}$ , we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {L}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {L}} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 2} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {L}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {L}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = \| (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {L}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {L}} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {L}} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \leq \| \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}. \\ \leq \left\| Y _ {\mathcal {Q}} Y _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} / n \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} / n + o _ {\mathbb {P}, d} (1) \cdot / n \\ = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {E} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 2} \boldsymbol {E} = \lim  _ {L \rightarrow \infty} \boldsymbol {E} _ {\mathcal {L}} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 2} \boldsymbol {E} _ {\mathcal {L}} \\ = \lim _ {L \to \infty} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {L}} ^ {\intercal} [ \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {L}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {L}} ^ {\intercal} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 2} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {L}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {L}} ] \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {L}} \\ \leq \| (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \cdot \lim _ {L \to \infty} \| \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {\mathcal {L}} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

which gives

$$
T _ {4} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \tau \| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \left(\tau^ {2} + \| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}\right). \tag {65}
$$

We decompose  $T_{5}$  using  $\pmb {f} = \pmb{f}_{\mathcal{Q}} + \pmb{f}_{\mathcal{Q}^c}$

$$
T _ {5} = T _ {5 1} + T _ {5 2},
$$

where

$$
T _ {5 1} = \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\mathsf {T}} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}},
$$

$$
T _ {5 2} = \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\mathsf {T}} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}}.
$$

First notice that

$$
\| M ^ {1 / 2} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 2} \boldsymbol {M} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\text {o p}} = \| \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} \left(\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}\right) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\text {o p}} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1).
$$

Then by Lemma 6, we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {\varepsilon}} [ T _ {5 1} ^ {2} ] / \tau^ {2} = \mathbb {E} _ {\boldsymbol {\varepsilon}} [ \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {\mathsf {T}} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ] / \tau^ {2} \\ = \pmb {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} [ (\pmb {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \pmb {M} (\pmb {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} ] ^ {2} \pmb {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \\ \leq \| \boldsymbol {M} ^ {1 / 2} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 2} \boldsymbol {M} ^ {1 / 2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| \boldsymbol {M} ^ {1 / 2} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {f} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot T _ {2 1} \\ = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Similarly, we get

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {\varepsilon} [ T _ {5 2} ^ {2} ] / \tau^ {2} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot T _ {2 3} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}.
$$

By Markov's inequality, we deduce that

$$
T _ {5} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \tau \left(\| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} + \| \mathrm {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}}\right) = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot \left(\tau^ {2} + \| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2}\right). \tag {66}
$$

# Step 6. Finish the proof.

Combining Eqs. (63), (59), (64), (65) and (66), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} R _ {\mathrm {K R R}} (f _ {d}, \boldsymbol {X}, \lambda) = \| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} - 2 T _ {1} + T _ {2} + T _ {3} - 2 T _ {4} + 2 T _ {5} \\ = \| \mathsf {P} _ {\mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} + o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1) \cdot (\| f _ {d} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} + \tau^ {2}), \\ \end{array}
$$

which concludes the proof.

# D.3 Auxiliary results

Lemma 3. Let  $\{Y_{\pmb{k},\pmb{s}}^{\pmb{d}}\}_{\pmb{k}\in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q},\pmb{s}\in [B(\pmb {d},\pmb {k})]}$  be the collection of tensor product of spherical harmonics on PSd. Let  $(\overline{x}_i)_{i\in [n]}\sim_{ii}$  Unif(PSd). Denote

$$
\pmb {Y} _ {\pmb {k}} = (Y _ {\pmb {k}, \pmb {s}} ^ {\pmb {d}} (\overline {{\pmb {x}}} _ {i})) _ {i \in [ n ], \pmb {s} \in [ B (\pmb {d}, \pmb {k}) ]} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times B (\pmb {d}, \pmb {k})}.
$$

Assume that  $n \geq w_d(d^\gamma \log d)$  and consider

$$
\mathcal {R} = \left\{\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q} \Big | \sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} \eta_ {q} k _ {q} <   m (\gamma) \right\}.
$$

Denote  $A = \sum_{\pmb{k} \in \mathcal{R}} B(\pmb{d}, \pmb{k})$  and

$$
\mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {R}} = \left(\mathbf {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}}\right) _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {R}} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times A}.
$$

Then we have

$$
\mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {R}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {R}} / n = \mathbf {I} _ {A} + \boldsymbol {\Delta},
$$

with  $\pmb{\Delta} \in \mathbb{R}^{A \times A}$  and  $\mathbb{E}[\|\pmb{\Delta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}] = o_d(1)$ .

Proof of Lemma 3. Let  $\Psi = \mathbf{Y}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}_{\mathcal{R}} / n\in \mathbb{R}^{A\times A}$ . We can rewrite  $\Psi$  as

$$
\Psi = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \boldsymbol {h} _ {i} \boldsymbol {h} _ {i} ^ {\top},
$$

where  $\pmb{h}_i = (Y_{\pmb{k},\pmb{s}}^d (\overline{\pmb{x}}_i))_{\pmb{k}\in \mathcal{R},\pmb{s}\in [\pmb {B}(\pmb {d},\pmb {k})]}\in \mathbb{R}^A$  . We use matrix Bernstein inequality. Denote  $\pmb {X}_i =$ $\pmb{h}_i\pmb{h}_i - \mathbf{I}_A\in \mathbb{R}^{A\times A}$  . Then we have  $\mathbb{E}[\pmb {X}_i] = \mathbf{0}$  , and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \| \boldsymbol {h} _ {i} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + 1 = \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {R}} \sum_ {\boldsymbol {s} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k}) ]} Y _ {\boldsymbol {k}, \boldsymbol {s}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i}) ^ {2} + 1 \\ = \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {R}} B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k}) Q _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(\left\{\left\langle \overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i} ^ {(q)}, \overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i} ^ {(q)} \right\rangle \right\} _ {q \in [ Q ]}\right) + 1 = A + 1, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we use formula (22) and the normalization  $Q_{\pmb{k}}^{\pmb{d}}(d_1,\dots,d_Q) = 1$ . Denote  $V = \| \sum_{i=1}^{n} \mathbb{E}[X_i^2] \|_{\mathrm{op}}$ . Then we have

$$
V = n \| \mathbb {E} [ (\pmb {h} _ {i} \pmb {h} _ {i} ^ {\top} - \mathbf {I} _ {A}) ^ {2} ] \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = n \| \mathbb {E} [ \pmb {h} _ {i} \pmb {h} _ {i} ^ {\top} \pmb {h} _ {i} \pmb {h} _ {i} ^ {\top} - 2 \pmb {h} _ {i} \pmb {h} _ {i} ^ {\top} + \mathbf {I} _ {A} ] \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = n \| (A - 1) \mathbf {I} _ {A} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = n (A - 1),
$$

where we used  $\pmb{h}_i^\top \pmb{h}_i = \| \pmb{h}_i\| _2^2 = A$  and  $\mathbb{E}[h_i(\overline{x}_i)h_i^\top (\overline{x}_i)] = (\mathbb{E}[Y_{k,s}^d (\overline{x}_i)Y_{k',s'}^d (\overline{x}_i)])_{k s,k's'} = \mathbf{I}_A$ . As a result, we have for any  $t > 0$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\Psi} - \mathbf {I} _ {A} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \geq t\right) \leq A \exp \left\{- n ^ {2} t ^ {2} / \left[ 2 n (A - 1) + 2 (A + 1) n t / 3 \right] \right\} \\ \leq \exp \left\{- (n / A) t ^ {2} / [ 1 0 (1 + t) ] + \log A \right\}. \tag {67} \\ \end{array}
$$

Notice that there exists  $C > 0$  such that  $A \leq C \max_{\pmb{k} \in \mathcal{R}} \prod_{q \in [Q]} d^{\eta_q k_q} \leq C d^\gamma$  (by definition of  $m(\gamma)$  and  $\mathcal{R}$ ) and therefore  $n \geq w_d(A \log A)$ . Integrating the tail bound (67) proves the lemma.

Lemma 4. Let  $\sigma$  be an activation function satisfying Assumption 1. Let  $w_{d}(d^{\gamma}\log d)\leq n\leq O_{d}(d^{m(\gamma) - \delta})$  for some  $\gamma >0$  and  $\delta >0$ . Then there exists sequences  $\kappa_{h}$  and  $\kappa_{u}$  such that

$$
\boldsymbol {H} = \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} = \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} + \kappa_ {h} \left(\mathbf {I} _ {n} + \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h}\right), \tag {68}
$$

$$
M = \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} D _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} = \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} D _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} + \kappa_ {m} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {m}, \tag {69}
$$

where  $\kappa_{h} = \Theta_{d}(1)$ ,  $\kappa_{m} = O_{d}(d^{-m(\gamma)})$ ,  $\| \pmb{\Delta}_{h}\|_{\mathrm{op}} = o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$  and  $\| \pmb{\Delta}_m\|_{\mathrm{op}} = O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$ .

Proof of Lemma 4. Define

$$
\mathcal {R} = \left\{\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q} \Big | \sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} \eta_ {q} k _ {q} <   m (\gamma) \right\},
$$

$$
\mathcal {S} = \left\{\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathbb {Z} _ {\geq 0} ^ {Q} \Big | \sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} \eta_ {q} k _ {q} \geq m (\gamma) \right\},
$$

such that  $\mathcal{R} \cup \mathcal{S} = \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}$ . The proof comes from bounding the eigenvalues of the matrix  $Y_{k}Y_{k}^{\top}$  for  $\pmb{k} \in \mathcal{R}$  and  $\pmb{k} \in \mathcal{S}$  separately. From Corollary 1, we have

$$
\sup  _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {S}} \| \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} / B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k}) - \mathbf {I} _ {n} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1).
$$

Hence, we can write

$$
\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {S}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} = \kappa_ {h} \left(\mathbf {I} _ {n} + \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h, 1}\right), \tag {70}
$$

with  $\kappa_{h} = \sum_{\pmb{k}\in \mathcal{S}}\lambda_{\pmb{k}}^{\pmb{d}}(h_{\pmb{d}})B(\pmb {d},\pmb {k}) = O_{d}(1)$ . From Assumption 1.(b) and a proof similar to Lemma 20, there exists  $\pmb {k} = (0,\dots ,k,\dots ,0)$  (for  $k > L$  at position  $q_{\xi}$ ) such that  $\lim \inf_{d\to \infty}\lambda_k^d (h_d)B(d,k) > 0$ . Hence,  $\kappa_{h} = \Theta_{d}(1)$ .

From Lemma 3 we have for  $\pmb{k} \in \mathcal{R} \cap \mathcal{Q}^c$ ,

$$
\pmb {Y} _ {\pmb {k}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} \pmb {Y} _ {\pmb {k}} / n = \mathbf {I} _ {B (\pmb {d}, \pmb {k})} + \pmb {\Delta},
$$

with  $\| \pmb{\Delta}\|_{\mathrm{op}} = o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$ . We deduce that  $\| Y_kY_k^\top \|_{\mathrm{op}} = O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n)$ . Hence,

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\text {o p}} = O _ {d, \mathbb {P}} \left(n \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}\right)\right) = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1),
$$

where we used Lemma 2. We deduce that

$$
\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {R} \cap \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} = \kappa_ {h} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h, 2}, \tag {71}
$$

with  $\| \pmb{\Delta}_{h,2}\|_{\mathrm{op}} = o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$  where we used  $\kappa_h^{-1} = O_d(1)$ . Combining Eqs. (70) and (71) yields Eq. (68). Similarly, we get

$$
\sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} = \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {R} \cap \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} [ \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {d} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) ^ {2} n ] \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} / n + \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {S}} [ \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {d} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) ^ {2} B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k}) ] \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\top} / B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k}).
$$

Using Lemma 2, we have  $\lambda_{\pmb{k}}^{\pmb{d}}(h_{\pmb{d}})^{2}n\leq Cd^{-2m(\gamma)}n = O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(d^{-m(\gamma)})$  and  $\lambda_{\pmb{k}}^{\pmb{d}}(h_{\pmb{d}})^{2}B(\pmb {d},\pmb {k})\leq C\lambda_{\pmb{k}}^{\pmb{d}}(h_{\pmb{d}})\leq$ $C^\prime d^{-m(\gamma)}$ . Hence Eq. (69) is verified with

$$
\kappa_ {m} = \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {R} \cap \mathcal {Q} ^ {c}} \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) ^ {2} n + \sum_ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {S}} \lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}) ^ {2} B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k}).
$$

![](images/7170051e1e4165420f488fc4e1a9fdd614f245353b30416f2c2457f05c8592b6.jpg)

Lemma 5. Let  $\{Y_{\pmb{k},\pmb{s}}^{\pmb{d}}\}_{\pmb{k}\in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q},\pmb{s}\in [B(\pmb {d},\pmb {k})]}$  be the collection of product of spherical harmonics on  $L^2 (\mathrm{PS}^d,\mu_d)$ . Let  $(\overline{x}_i)_{i\in [n]}\sim_{iid}\mathrm{Unif}(\mathrm{PS}^d)$ . Denote

$$
\mathbf {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} = \left(Y _ {\boldsymbol {k}, \boldsymbol {s}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i})\right) _ {i \in [ n ], \boldsymbol {s} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k}) ]} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k})}.
$$

Then for  $\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{t}\in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}$  and  $\pmb {u}\neq \pmb{v}$  , we have

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {u}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {t}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {t}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {v}} \right] = \boldsymbol {0}.
$$

For  $\mathbf{u},\mathbf{t}\in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q}$  , we have

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {u}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {t}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {t}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {u}} \right] = \left[ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {t}) n + n (n - 1) \delta_ {\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {t}} \right] \mathbf {I} _ {B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {u})}.
$$

Proof. We have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ Y _ {u} ^ {\top} Y _ {t} Y _ {t} ^ {\top} Y _ {v} \right] \\ = \sum_ {i, j \in [ n ]} \sum_ {\boldsymbol {m} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, t) ]} \left(\mathbb {E} \left[ Y _ {\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {p}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i}) \left(Y _ {\boldsymbol {t}, \boldsymbol {m}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i}) Y _ {\boldsymbol {t}, \boldsymbol {m}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {j})\right) Y _ {\boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {q}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {j}) \right]\right) _ {\boldsymbol {p} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {u}) ], \boldsymbol {q} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {v}) ]} \\ = \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \left(\mathbb {E} \left[ Y _ {\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {p}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i}) \left(\sum_ {\boldsymbol {m} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, t) ]} Y _ {\boldsymbol {t}, \boldsymbol {m}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i}) Y _ {\boldsymbol {t}, \boldsymbol {m}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i})\right) Y _ {\boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {q}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} (\overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} _ {i}) \right]\right) _ {\boldsymbol {p} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {u}) ], \boldsymbol {q} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {v}) ]} \\ + \sum_ {i \neq j \in [ n ]} \sum_ {\boldsymbol {m} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, t) ]} \left(\mathbb {E} \left[ Y _ {\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {p}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(\bar {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {i}\right) Y _ {\boldsymbol {t}, \boldsymbol {m}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(\bar {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {i}\right) Y _ {\boldsymbol {t}, \boldsymbol {m}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(\bar {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j}\right) Y _ {\boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {q}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(\bar {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j}\right) \right]\right) _ {\boldsymbol {p} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {u}) ], \boldsymbol {q} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {v}) ]} \tag {72} \\ = B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {t}) \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \left(\mathbb {E} \left[ Y _ {\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {p}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(\bar {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {i}\right) Y _ {\boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {q}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(\bar {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {i}\right) \right]\right) _ {\boldsymbol {p} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {u}) ], \boldsymbol {q} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {v}) ]} \\ + \sum_ {i \neq j \in [ n ]} \sum_ {\boldsymbol {m} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, t) ]} \left(\delta_ {\boldsymbol {u}, t} \delta_ {\boldsymbol {p}, \boldsymbol {m}} \delta_ {\boldsymbol {t}, \boldsymbol {v}} \delta_ {\boldsymbol {q}, \boldsymbol {m}}\right) _ {\boldsymbol {p} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {u}) ], \boldsymbol {q} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {v}) ]} \\ = (B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {t}) n \delta_ {\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {v}} \delta_ {\boldsymbol {p}, \boldsymbol {q}} + n (n - 1) \delta_ {\boldsymbol {u}, \boldsymbol {t}} \delta_ {\boldsymbol {t}, \boldsymbol {v}} \delta_ {\boldsymbol {p}, \boldsymbol {q}}) _ {\boldsymbol {p} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {u}) ], \boldsymbol {q} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {v}) ]}. \\ \end{array}
$$

This proves the lemma.

Lemma 6. Let  $\sigma$  be an activation function satisfying Assumption 1. Assume  $\omega_{d}(d^{\gamma}\log d)\leq n\leq O_{d}(d^{m(\gamma) - \delta})$  for some  $\gamma >0$  and  $\delta >0$ . We have

$$
\| n (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} - \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\top} / n \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1).
$$

Proof of Lemma 6. Denote

$$
\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}} = \left(Y _ {\boldsymbol {k}, \boldsymbol {s}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(\bar {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {i}\right)\right) _ {i \in [ n ], \boldsymbol {s} \in [ B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k}) ]} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k})}. \tag {73}
$$

Denote  $B = \sum_{\pmb{k} \in \mathcal{Q}} B(\pmb{d}, \pmb{k})$ , and

$$
\mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} = \left(\mathbf {Y} _ {\boldsymbol {k}}\right) _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q}} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times B},
$$

and

$$
\boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} = \operatorname {d i a g} \left(\left(\lambda_ {\boldsymbol {k}} ^ {\boldsymbol {d}} \left(h _ {\boldsymbol {d}}\right) \mathbf {I} _ {B (\boldsymbol {d}, \boldsymbol {k})}\right) _ {\boldsymbol {k} \in \mathcal {Q}}\right) \in \mathbb {R} ^ {B \times B}.
$$

From Lemma 4, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} n (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {M} (\boldsymbol {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \\ = n (\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} + (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) \mathbf {I} _ {n} + \kappa_ {h} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h}) ^ {- 1} (\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} + \kappa_ {m} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {m}) (\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} + (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) \mathbf {I} _ {n} + \kappa_ {h} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h}) ^ {- 1} \\ = T _ {1} + T _ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\| \pmb{\Delta}_h\|_{\mathrm{op}} = o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$ ,  $\| \pmb{\Delta}_u\|_{\mathrm{op}} = O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$  and  $\kappa_{m} = O_{d}(d^{-m(\gamma)})$ , and

$$
T _ {1} = n \kappa_ {m} (\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\intercal} + (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) \mathbf {I} _ {n} + \kappa_ {h} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {m} (\boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \boldsymbol {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\intercal} + (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) \mathbf {I} _ {n} + \kappa_ {h} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h}) ^ {- 1},
$$

$$
T _ {2} = n \left(\mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \mathbf {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} + (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) \mathbf {I} _ {n} + \kappa_ {h} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \mathbf {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {2} \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} \left(\mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \mathbf {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} + (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) \mathbf {I} _ {n} + \kappa_ {h} \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {h}\right) ^ {- 1}.
$$

Then, we can use the same proof as in [GMMM19b, Lemma 13] to bound  $\| T_1\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  (recall  $n = O_d(d^{m(\gamma) - \delta})$ )

$$
\left\| T _ {1} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq 2 n \kappa_ {m} / (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) ^ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {\Delta} _ {m} \right\| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1),
$$

and  $\| T_2 - Y_{\mathcal{Q}}Y_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top} / n\|_{\mathrm{op}} = o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)$ , where we only need to check that

$$
\lambda_ {\min} (\pmb {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} / [ (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) / n ]) = \min _ {\pmb {k} \in \mathcal {Q}} [ n \lambda_ {\pmb {k}} ^ {\pmb {d}} (h _ {\pmb {d}}) ] / (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) = w _ {d} (1),
$$

which directly follows from Lemma 2.

Lemma 7. Let  $\sigma$  be an activation function satisfying Assumption 1. Assume  $\omega_{d}(d^{\gamma}\log d)\leq n\leq O_{d}(d^{m(\gamma) - \delta})$  for some  $\gamma >0$  and  $\delta >0$ . We have

$$
\| \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} ^ {\mathsf {T}} (\mathbf {H} + \lambda \mathbf {I} _ {n}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {Y} _ {\mathcal {Q}} \mathbf {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} - \mathbf {I} _ {B} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} = o _ {d, \mathbb {P}} (1).
$$

Proof of Lemma 7.

This lemma can be deduced directly from [GMMM19b, Lemma 14], by noticing that

$$
\lambda_ {\min} (\pmb {D} _ {\mathcal {Q}} / [ (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) / n ] = \min _ {\pmb {k} \in \mathcal {Q}} [ n \lambda_ {\pmb {k}} ^ {\pmb {d}} (h _ {\pmb {d}}) ] / (\kappa_ {h} + \lambda) = \omega_ {d} (1),
$$

from Lemma 2.

# E Proof of Theorem 6.(a): lower bound for the RF model

# E.1 Preliminaries

In the theorems, we show our results in high probability with respect to  $\Theta$ . Hence, in the proof we will restrict the sample space to the high probability event  $\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon} \equiv \mathcal{P}_{d,N,\varepsilon}$  for  $\varepsilon > 0$  small enough, where

$$
\mathcal {P} _ {d, N, \varepsilon} \equiv \left\{\Theta \mid \tau_ {i} ^ {(q)} \in [ 1 - \varepsilon , 1 + \varepsilon ], \forall i \in [ N ], \forall q \in [ Q ] \right\} \subset \left(\mathbb {S} ^ {D - 1} (\sqrt {D})\right) ^ {\otimes N}. \tag {74}
$$

We will denote  $\mathbb{E}_{\pmb{\tau}_{\varepsilon}}$  the expectation over  $\pmb{\tau}$  restricted to  $\tau^{(q)}\in [1 - \varepsilon ,1 + \varepsilon ]$  for all  $q\in [Q]$ , and  $\mathbb{E}_{\Theta_{\varepsilon}}$  the expectation over  $\Theta$  restricted to the event  $\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}$ .

Lemma 8. Assume  $N = o(d^{\gamma})$  for some  $\gamma >0$ . We have for any fixed  $\varepsilon >0$

$$
\mathbb {P} \left(\mathcal {P} _ {\varepsilon} ^ {c}\right) = o _ {d} (1).
$$

Proof of Lemma 8. The tail inequality in Lemma 16 and the assumption  $N = o(d^{\gamma})$  imply that there exists some constants  $C, c > 0$  such that

$$
\mathbb {P} \left(\mathcal {P} _ {d, N, \varepsilon} ^ {c}\right) \leq \sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} N \mathbb {P} \left(\left| \tau^ {(q)} - 1 \right| > \varepsilon\right) \leq \sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} C \exp \left(\gamma \log (d) - c d ^ {\eta_ {q}} \varepsilon\right) = o _ {d} (1).
$$

![](images/ba76974b464b9bcb2238bdd5c01840a23bde87a070d6745b7a49c9010f1dd8eb.jpg)

We consider the activation function  $\sigma : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ . Let  $\pmb{\theta} \sim \mathbb{S}^{D-1}(\sqrt{D})$  and  $\pmb{x} = \{\pmb{x}^{(q)}\}_{q \in [Q]} \in \mathrm{PS}_{\kappa}^{\pmb{d}}$ . We introduce the function  $\sigma_{\pmb{d},\tau} : \mathrm{ps}^{\pmb{d}} \to \mathbb{R}$  such that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {x} \right\rangle / R\right) = \sigma \left(\sum_ {q \in [ Q ]} \tau^ {(q)} \cdot \left(r _ {q} / R\right) \cdot \left\langle \overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}} ^ {(q)}, \overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} ^ {(q)} \right\rangle / \sqrt {d _ {q}}\right) \tag {75} \\ \equiv \sigma_ {\boldsymbol {d}, \tau} \left(\{\langle \overline {{\boldsymbol {\theta}}} ^ {(q)}, \overline {{\boldsymbol {x}}} ^ {(q)} \rangle / \sqrt {d _ {q}} \} _ {q \in [ Q ]}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$
